# output:
You're apprenticed to one book, and your job is to bring its teaching into our painterly pixel-art medium for voxsim, a simulation of Washington State around 1700: **Rex Vicat Cole, *The Artistic Anatomy of Trees*, with Hallé, Oldeman & Tomlinson, *Tropical Trees and Forests: An Architectural Analysis***.

The file is at ~/work/pixelart-studies/library/trees/artisticanatomyo1915cole.pdf and ~/work/pixelart-studies/library/purchased/Tropical trees and forests  an architectural analysis (Hallé, Francis, Hallé-Oldeman).pdf. Hallé's text is in ~/work/pixelart-studies/library/text/.

Two complementary model systems. Cole shows a painter how each tree's form follows from its structure: branching by species, how foliage masses group, bark, silhouettes and seasons, in 300 diagrams. Hallé–Oldeman give the botanist's architectural models: a small number of growth plans that generate a species' whole form, and how architecture changes as a tree ages and reiterates.

Where it matters: Washington conifers and broadleaves (Douglas-fir, hemlock, cedar, Sitka spruce, subalpine fir, larch, alder, maple, madrone, Garry oak) in the canopy and treeline studies, and in voxsim's tree species. Those are now data in the content packs (~/work/voxsim-wt/platform/docs/PACKS.md), so your models could become how species are specified.

**Why books.** The studio's best work (the elk and deer study, ~/work/artschool/posts/deer-elk/) succeeded by building on finished expert analysis: Muybridge's plates, a naturalist's measured guide. Natural scientists and naturalist painters spent careers building model systems of how nature looks and how to draw it economically and truthfully. Your book is one of those. Read it as an apprentice, not a skimmer.

**The work:**

1. **Read the whole book closely, and look at every plate and diagram.** The drawings are the teaching. Write down the book's model: its principles, rules, procedures and vocabulary, in its own terms, with page references.
2. **Translate it into pixels, one principle at a time, bottom-up.** For each principle, do a small exercise:
   - master-copy the book's own diagram or plate at our scales (close scene ~0.1 m/px, landscape, 30° iso, region ~1 m/px);
   - then find the pixel method that carries the same truth;
   - compare at 8× against the book's plate and against real photos.

   Some principles won't carry, and some need rethinking for pixels. Say which and why.
3. **Test it where it matters.** Apply the method to the real subjects it's for (listed below), on real photos, on the place lanes' renders, or on other students' scenes, and show before | after beside the reference.
4. **Leave it usable.** The method as parameterised code, and a clear statement of the rules other students and the voxsim lanes can apply.

**The art school.** Read it first and mine it deeply (~/work/artschool/posts/, https://graunked.github.io/artschool/): its students' tutorials and process stories show how the good work happened. You're a me

# The studio library: model systems

The elk and deer student succeeded largely because it didn't start from raw photos. It started from finished analysis:

- Muybridge's frame-by-frame plates;
- the naturalist's "four things that make a 6-pixel elk read".

Natural scientists used to be trained to draw: a geologist could draw a flake, an outcrop or a fold with economy and truth. Naturalist painting guides were built on the same scientific understanding. This library collects those traditions so studies can start where the experts finished, instead of doing a geology PhD from photographs.

**How to use it:**

- Before drawing, find your subject's model system here (or online, below) and read it closely.
- Then build on it, the way the elk student built on Muybridge.
- Open the PDFs and look at the plates; the drawings are the point.

Refetch anything missing with `./fetch.sh` (resumable). Files are also mirrored in S3 under `voxsim/shared/library/`.

## On disk (open access)

| Domain | File | What it gives you |
|---|---|---|
| Terrain, landforms | `terrain/blockdiagramsoth0000armi_s8g3.pdf` | **Lobeck, *Block Diagrams* (1924).** How geologists draw landforms in 3-D: every landform type as a block diagram, with the drawing method. |
| Terrain, landforms | `terrain/2015.205632.Geomorphology.pdf` | **Lobeck, *Geomorphology* (1939).** Landforms explained through his own drawings: glacial troughs, cirques, rivers, coasts, volcanoes. |
| Terrain, landforms | `terrain/physicalgeograp00davigoog.pdf` | **W. M. Davis, *Physical Geography* (1898).** Davis's block diagrams: how landscapes form and age. |
| Terrain, rock at map scale | `terrain/2015.119840.Cartographic-Relief-Presentation.pdf` | **Imhof, *Cartographic Relief Presentation*.** The rigorous method for showing terrain by light, and Swiss rock drawing: cliffs rendered by structure with a few strokes. Probably the single most useful text for rock at landscape and region scale. |
| Rock, mountains | `rock/modernpainters04rusk.pdf` | **Ruskin, *Modern Painters IV: Of Mountain Beauty* (1856).** A painter's analysis of rock types, cleavage, crests, banks and stones, written for artists and illustrated with his own drawings. |
| Rock, strata at distance | `rock/holmes/*.jpg` | **William Henry Holmes, plates for the Grand Cañon Atlas (1882).** The masterpieces of American scientific landscape drawing: stratified cliffs and plateaus at great distance in a few values and stepped strata lines. Directly relevant to the Yakima basalt. |
| Rock, structure | `rock/reportongeology05duttgoog.pdf` | **G. K. Gilbert, *Geology of the Henry Mountains* (1877).** A classic illustrated geological report. |
| Rock, a flake | `rock/cifa_lithic_illustration_guide.pdf` | **CIfA, *The Illustration of Lithic Artefacts*.** How archaeologists draw a knapped flake: conventions for ridges, ripples and light. The drawing-a-flake skill itself. |
| Trees | `trees/artisticanatomyo1915cole.pdf` | **Rex Vicat Cole, *The Artistic Anatomy

# Four giants, tier by tier

*The conifers student, writing the day after. The study ran one night, Friday 25 September 2026, from 23:00 to about 01:30: 2 h 24 min of my own working time, 223 steps, 91 images looked at. Images numbered `nbNNN` are ones I opened while working, numbered as in [the notebook](../../notebooks/conifers/notebook.md). Several were overwritten on disk later, so the notebook is the only place they survive. Images named `t…` were made today by [`code/figs.py`](code/figs.py) with the study's own painter, to show things I only half-saw that night. What I learned, shaped for teaching, is in [TUTORIAL.md](TUTORIAL.md).*

The brief: learn to paint the great Pacific Northwest conifers — Douglas-fir, western hemlock, western redcedar and Sitka spruce — as individuals and as forest, in Mark Ferrari's pixel language. See how far a student can go past voxsim's existing trees, which David had called perfect. The subject list was specific: hemlock's drooping leader and weeping sprays, cedar's buttressed, fluted base and fanned sprays, old Douglas-fir's deep-furrowed bark and layered crown, and the stiff blue-green rainforest spruce. It had to be delivered as code with parameters, plus labelled sheets sweeping species × age × hour × distance × seed.

The short version. The best thing I made is a mixed stand at real heights, relit across four hours by nothing but a palette swap.

![sheet E](img/E_stand_x_hour.png)

*Sheet E: one index canvas, four palettes. The second critic called golden and dusk "the studio's best forest images".*

The technique that made it work is one line of arithmetic. It came from a critic's first note, and I'd have missed it on my own for a long time: **light the tops of the tiers, not the sun-side half of the tree.** The things that didn't work are at the end: the iso forest, and two of the four barks.

---

## 1. Before painting: an hour of reading

I read before I touched a pixel, because the studio had done a lot of work already and I'd rather steal than rediscover.

**The willow student** (`elements/willow/LESSONS.md` and `CRITIQUE.md`) was the model I was pointed at. It went master copy, then a single bush in grey, then stems, leaves, colour and clumps. Two things from it shaped my night:

- **The clump grammar.** Foliage is a heap of lit-capped clumps, and the clumps are laid out as a light field, never painted directly.
- **The critic's steer in its round 5:** judge a leaf's form against photos of the plant, and use Ferrari only for craft. That's now the studio's rule. It freed me to stop copying his spruce once I'd learned its grammar.

**SYNTHESIS.md** had a line on conifers from master copy's C1: "1-px spire, drooping spurs lengthening downward, lit dabs on sun-side tips." The master-copy LESSONS gave numbers: a 1-px spire for about 4 rows, tiers every 4–5 rows, half-width about 1 + 0.15·d, 15–20% holes. The analysis student's material table had "conifer: black 61%, ochre fleck 22%, dark 14%

# Ready to use: the second studio (2026-09-26 night)

These are the techniques the second studio critic judged Ferrari-grade or ready to use. The critic ran 10 rounds on eight students, looking at 4–10× crops beside the students' photos and Ferrari's own pixels. Each item points to the student's folder, where the code, the lessons and the closing read in CRITIQUE.md live. Offered to the voxsim painting lanes as a resource, not a spec. None of the eight sets is at Ferrari's bar at 8× across the board; every student's "Where it stands" says so, and I agree with each of them.

**Masses first, for ground** (elements/ground: LESSONS "The rules to take even without the code"; sheets B and F; drop-in `ground.paint_ground(..., shadow_mask=)`). This is the studio's first answer to READY.md's main open problem.

- Decide two to four designed lit and shadow masses first, from off-frame casters and the landform.
- Keep shadow near black with 2–3 values, and put the colour in the light.
- Marks go inside a mass and never cross the terminator, except as a fringe of tips.
- Use ordered 2×2 dither for mid and far turns. Use runs only near.
- Best images: `sheets/F_scenes.png` (meadow bank, golden) and `sheets/B_landform_x_sun.png` (hummocks and contre-jour).

**Zoom handoff without pops** (elements/grass-lod: LESSONS §1–2, 6). Each tuft gets one stroke-size number, and every discrete choice is dithered against the tuft's own random numbers. Checked frame by frame across 100× zoom at four hours. The pan test and pop metric are reusable for any material.

**Conifers** (elements/conifers; best: `sheets/E_stand_x_hour.png`, `F_zoom_strips_1x.png`)

- Four PNW species at four ages with naturalist-grade form: nodding hemlock, cedar J-limbs with fluted buttress and candelabra, spruce curtains, the old Douglas-fir pillar.
- **Tier-top light** from V16: light on the upper surface of every tier on both sides of the stem, not on the sun-side half.
- Side-view stand relit at four hours by palette alone.

**Broadleaves** (elements/broadleaf; best: `stage5/contact_v7.png`, sheet A)

- Pacific madrone and Garry oak crowns. The oak comes from per-species elbow kinks and open crowns with limbs showing in the holes.
- Bark and moss: the checker-banded cylinder read from V30 ("pure / 50% / pure, never non-adjacent steps"), a moss sheath with drips and a seam, madrone peel.
- An LOD rule with no vanishing: a clump always emits a spray, and a spray always emits a mark.
- Phenology specific to each species.

**Sagebrush steppe** (elements/steppe; best: `hero/sage_flat_golden_3x.png`, `stage5/seasons_final`)

- Th

   THE NEW ART LIBRARY
 Edited by M. H.   SPIELMANN, F.S.A., &- P. G. KONODY




THE ARTISTIC ANATOMY OF TREES
Plate I

                         Pencil Study by the Author

                             PREFACE
In teaching, as I understand it, one must assume that the student
knows nothing.    When one has overcome the disagreeable feeling that
this assumption implies, that of being set up on one's own little pedestal,
one is able to be of more use than if one attempted to dovetail bits of
knowledge into those that have already been acquired. This is more
particularly the case when compiling a book that has to meet the
needs of students in various stages of proficiency.        My standpoint,
then, for which I must apologise, is that the reader knows but little
of draughtsmanship, and nothing of the construction of trees.
    I cannot understand the position of some who assert that an object
can be painted in a convincing manner without its mechanism being
understood. Would they come through the test of having to draw a
yacht or a locomotive, or would they shuffle out of the difficulties by
saying they only wished to give the " feeling " that impressed them ?
I doubt whether the feeling alone would please the yachtsman, mechanic,
or artist.   At this stage I shall be accused of saying that correctness
is the chief aim of the artist, when it is but a little part.  Most of the
greatest artists, if judged by the photographic standard, have drawn
incorrectly.                                    —
               This is a fact I delight in, since Gloving their work and
                                                 —
yet confessing to a certain pleasure in accuracy ^I find I am sufficient
artist myself to recognise those finer qualities that can dispense with
exactness without loss of merit.    Sailors tell me that Turner's " Fight-
ing Temeraire " could not have fought

# output:
The Layered Forest Chapter 5 Forests and Vegetation A. The Architecture of Forest Plots Architecture is a concept that can be applied not only to individual plants or their parts, but to all living systems. For example, it has been used by Rollet (1974) to indicate the population charac¬ teristics of forests, whereby mathematical relationships are expressed graphically. In our use of the term, architecture has been referred to momentary form com¬ plexes, and the processes which determine the change from one architectural phase to another have been represented by a growth model. It is useful to speak of forest architecture and sylvigenetic model by analogy with tree architecture and the tree model, with reference to their differ¬ ences of scale and controlling factors. In a single tree, the model includes a set of axes organized according to the differenti¬ ation sequence. This sequence is what the tree has in excess of the mere sum of its axes, i.e., what distinguishes it from an arbitrary collection of axes. At the scale of the forest, the model includes a set of trees aggregated as vegetation by inter¬ action between a diversified set of trees. This coherent diversification, which has been expounded in the preceding sections on trees of the present, the future and the past, is what the forest has in excess of the mere sum of its trees, i.e., what distinguishes it from a collection of arbi¬ trarily chosen trees. In the present chapter we look at forest architecture of an area sufficiently small to be capable of analysis because it repre¬ sents a uniform architectural phase. Our method is quite unstatistical, just as it was for individual trees, where not the aver¬ age, but the rare tree was studied, con¬ forming to and so revealing its initial mo¬ del. Intact architecture is less easy to de¬ tect at first sight in forests than in individ¬ ual trees, and indeed, of the examples presented later in this chapter nearly all show some aberrant features. In this respect, it might have been some help to study tree plantations which are artificially homogeneous, but no such cul¬ tures were established in French Guiana where the analytical method originated. Some of the schematic diagrams from the treatise on temperate sylviculture by Houtzagers (1956), representing techni¬ cal sketches of planted forest before thin¬ ning or harvesting, effectively correspond to simplified versions of the natural tem¬ perate forest, e.g., that represented by Figure 102. The size of the natural forest plots examined in this chapter has been empiri¬ cally determined, and coincides rather well with the smallest sample (plot or “station”) in statistically oriented vegeta¬ tion analysis as for example by Godron (1971), although here we do not establish plot size by a statistical method. # output:
arrangement confers dorsiventral symmetry. In many trees the phyllotaxis of the adult parts is constant for all shoots; erect shoots with a spiral or decussate leaf arrangement preserve the radial symmetry of their primary leaf orientation but may lose it on their horizontal shoots by secondary orientation or by differential growth of leaves (e.g., Anisophyllea disticha) (Fig. 11) so that we may speak of secondary dorsiventrality, a topic discussed in detail by Massart (1923). These secondary changes which can reorientate either the blade or the whole leaf can be due to twisting of petioles (e.g., Coffea), to differential elongation of petioles (Acer), or to the activity of pulvini (e.g., Theobroma and other Sterculiaceae, many Leguminosae) or to the twisting of internodes (horizontal shoots with decussate leaves; many Myrtaceae) or from various combinations of these processes. Individual leaves then become orientated into a position appropriate for presumed maximum photosynthetic activity, i.e., at right angles to the incident light, which in the forest is mainly from above. The leaves on horizontal shoots are then always arranged in one plane, regardless of their primary orientation in the bud. Secondary dorsiventrality is achieved very commonly in many members of the Rubiaceae in which leaves are predominantly decussate (but sometimes verticillate, i.e., whorled), but in which all leaf pairs on horizontal shoots rotate into one plane by twisting of internodes, as in Coffea. A series of illustrations which show this realignment in Tachia guianensis Aubl. (Gentianaceae) is shown in Figure 3 A, B. In the gymnosperms a spiral or whorled leaf arrangement is constant, but on horizontal shoots a marked dorsiventrality is achieved by secondary leaf orientation (Abies, Picea), sometimes with the addiChapter 2 Branching: Dynamics Elements of Tree Architecture tion of anisophylly (e.g., Tsuga canadensis). Radial symmetry is sometimes reached by an unusual phyllotaxis. In monocotyledons the distichy is often twisted (spirodistichous), this being so pronounced in species of Cordyline (Agavaceae) that a radial symmetry results. In Pandanus the phyllotaxis is essentially but this is not precise and mutual shading of leaves is reduced by additional twist, hence the common name “screw-pine” for such plants. Distichous leaves are rare in the orthotropic shoots of woody monocotyledons, but where they occur and are large the shoots are very striking (e.g., Ravenala and other Strelitziaceae; a few palms like Oenocarpus distichus and Wallichia disticha). In many bamboos the tall culms have a distichous leaf arrangement. Distichy is uncommon in erect shoots of dicotyledons; Annonaceae possibly provide exceptions, others are to be looked for in young Campnosperma trees (Anacardiaceae), most of the species of the genus Erythroxylon (Erythroxylaceae), and some Myrtaceae. import sys, os
from PIL import Image, ImageDraw
src, dst, a, b = sys.argv[1], sys.argv[2], int(sys.argv[3]), int(sys.argv[4])
n = int(sys.argv[5]) if len(sys.argv) > 5 else 3
for s in range(a, b + 1, n):
    ims = []
    for p in range(s, min(s + n, b + 1)):
        f = f"{src}/p-{p:03d}.jpg"
        if os.path.exists(f):
            ims.append((p, Image.open(f).convert('L')))
    if not ims: continue
    w, h = ims[0][1].size
    sheet = Image.new('L', (w * len(ims) + 8 * (len(ims) - 1), h + 30), 255)
    d = ImageDraw.Draw(sheet)
    for i, (p, im) in enumerate(ims):
        im = im.resize((w, h))
        sheet.paste(im, (i * (w + 8), 30))
        d.text((i * (w + 8) + 10, 8), f"pdf p{p}", fill=0)
    sheet.save(f"{dst}/s{s:03d}.jpg", quality=80)

from PIL import Image, ImageDraw
import os
def grid(pages,out,cols=6,w=330):
    ims=[(p,Image.open(f'pages/cole/p-{p:03d}.jpg')) for p in pages if os.path.exists(f'pages/cole/p-{p:03d}.jpg')]
    h=int(w*926/617)
    rows=(len(ims)+cols-1)//cols
    S=Image.new('L',(cols*w,rows*(h+14)),255);d=ImageDraw.Draw(S)
    for i,(p,im) in enumerate(ims):
        x=(i%cols)*w;y=(i//cols)*(h+14)
        S.paste(im.resize((w-4,h)),(x,y+14));d.text((x+4,y+1),str(p),fill=0)
    S.save(out,quality=85)
grid([46,47,48,49,50,51,53,54,55,56,59,60],'tmp/plates1.jpg')

# The great Pacific Northwest conifers, after Mark Ferrari: lessons

A student's log. Subject: Douglas-fir, western hemlock, western redcedar and Sitka spruce, as
individuals and as forest, from 1-cm needles to a 2.5 m/px iso region.

**Code** (all in this folder; numpy only, `nice -n 10`, one BLAS thread):

| file | what it is |
|---|---|
| `grow.py` | the four species grown as geometry in metres: trunk (lean, taper, buttress flare, flutes, hemlock's nodding leader), limbs in whorls or scattered with species droop / J-hook upturn, secondary *fans* on mature and old limbs, old-crown gaps, dead stubs, cedar candelabra leaders and silver spike tops. Species and age tables (`SP`, `AGE`) are the parameters. |
| `foliage_fast.py` | the foliage *elements* (fronds, strands, brush tufts, spurs, needle fill, coarse-scale area fill), vectorized (10-25x faster than the loop version `grow._foliage_slow`, which it matches; `tmp/vectorize_check.png`). |
| `paint.py` | the painter: light per foliage point, rasterize body + stroke marks, value plan, quantize to an index canvas (material, step), wood surfaces with bark patterns. |
| `palette.py` | the palette is the light: 8-step foliage ramps (shadow family 0-3, lit family 4-7) per species x hour x aerial depth; wood, ground and sky ramps. |
| `conifer.py` | public API: `tree(...)`, `stand(...)` (depth-composited stands with one shared shadow map and a ground plane with cast shadows). |
| `sheets.py` | the labelled parameter sheets in `sheets/`. |
| `tree.py`, `render.py`, `s0_copy.py`, `s0_tune.py`, `stats.py`, `lw.py` | stage 0 (the master copy), frozen as its regression. |
| `s1_*.py` ... `s5_bark.py` | one script per study stage; `cmp.py` side-by-side helpers; `cmp_voxtrees.py`. |

Photos: `refs/` (fetched with the studio's imgfetch; each folder has a `_sheet.jpg` contact
sheet). The ones I leaned on: the Hoh hemlock against sky
(`Tsuga_heterophylla/commons_Hoh_Rainforest_...`), the open-grown Sitka spruce
(`Douglas_fir_tree_crown/openverse_Sitka_Spruce_b36ebb43.jpg`), Big Lonely Doug
(`lone_Douglas_fir_tree/openverse_Big_Lonely_Doug_97f10fa9.jpg`), the ancient cedar
(`western_redcedar_candelabra_top/openverse_Thuja_plicata_..._ancient_form_...`), the Willapa cedar
bole, and the naturalist desk's guides (`~/work/naturalist/plants/02-trees.md`, `05-old-growth.md`),
which gave me real heights (mature 40-60 m, old 60-75 m), the bark and foliage colours, and the
single most useful sentence for a painter: *every hemlock's tip nods over like

## A species file



name = &quot;douglas fir&quot;          # the agent name; defaults to the file name, dashes as spaces
aliases = []                  # other names that resolve to it (renderer lookups)
based_on = &quot;spruce&quot;           # optional: start from another species (any loaded pack), override below

[sim]      # vox-flora: traits (vox_flora::species::Species)
[growth]   # vox-flora: the growth grammar of its skeleton (vox_flora::form::Arch)
[look]     # vox-trees: fallback structure generator + how it is painted (vox_trees::species::LookDoc)
[paint]    # vox-paint: sprite family for proportions / the non-vox-trees painter




Every section is optional; a species exists for a consumer only if it has
that consumer's section (a plant with `[sim]` but no `[look]` grows in the
sim and is drawn by vox-paint's own sprites, like meadows today).

`based_on` merges tables key by key (deep), so `based_on = "spruce"` plus
`[look.colour] leaf = "..."` changes only the needle colour. `name` and
`aliases` never inherit. Chains are fine; cycles are errors.

Units: the sim works in voxels (0.5 m) for heights and radii (`max_h = 120`
is 60 m), years for ages, metres per second, degrees Celsius. The look works
in metres and radians (it takes the tree's real size from the sim).

### `[sim]` (vox-flora traits)

The fields of `vox_flora::species::Species`, all optional except `form`,
defaults as in the source:

| field | meaning |
|---|---|
| `form` | `broadleaf`, `conifer`, `bush`, `herb`, `reed`, `meadow`, `moss`, `fungus`, `crop`, `aquatic`, `deadwood` |
| `max_h`, `max_cr` | mature height, crown radius (voxels) |
| `photo`, `t_opt`, `t_width`, `frost_active`, `frost_dormant` | growth rate multiplier, temperature optimum/width, frost limits (C) |
| `moist`, `wet_tol`, `shade_tol` | preferred soil moisture 0..1, waterlogging tolerance, shade tolerance |
| `deciduous`, `fruit`, `fruit_name` | `fruit` is an item (`"nuts"`, `"berries"`, `"seeds"`, ...) |
| `wind_seeds`, `spores`, `clonal`, `thorny`, `resinous`, `tannins`, `toxic`, `n_fix`, `browse`, `rock`, `acid` | flags |
| `alt` | relative altitude band `[lo, hi]` (0 shore .. 1 highest land) |
| `near_water` | max distance to water (voxels) for water-lovers, 0 = any |
| `lifespan_years`, `mature_years`, `spacing`, `abundance`, `color` | placement: min spacing (voxels), weight where suitable |
| `wood_density`, `rupture_mpa`, `rooting` | mechanics: green density kg/m3, modulus of rupture MPa, anchorage multiplier |
| `stump_sprouts`, `pioneer`, `mycorrhizal` | resprouts from a fallen trunk / stump; keeps 40% on young volcanic soil; hosts chanterelles |

### `[growth]` (vox-flora growth grammar)

The fields of `vox_flora::form::Arch` (Hallé-Oldeman style architecture:
habit, fork height, limbs, angles, gravitropism, branching, foliage clusters,
colours of the voxel body). Unset fields default per `form`
(`Arch::for_form`: conifers start excurrent, whorled, cone profile). The
base species files are the worked exam

name = "douglas fir"
# Pseudotsuga menziesii var. menziesii, coast Douglas-fir. The emergent of the
# west-side forest: a straight column to 60-75 m (old growth; 90+ m recorded),
# whorled near-level limbs with upswept tips, crown in the top third of a
# forest-grown tree above a long clear bole, bark deeply furrowed, corky,
# dark red-brown. Old trees lose the spire: an irregular, flattened top of a
# few big limbs. Sim values after lane-place-skykomish's research (naturalist
# plants/02-trees.md; Burns & Honkala 1990; Van Pelt 2001).

[sim]
form = "conifer"
max_h = 150.0            # voxels: 75 m
max_cr = 20.0            # 10 m crown radius
t_opt = 15.0
frost_active = -8.0

moist = 0.45

Bash: cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/trees; python3 - <<'E' from PIL import Image, ImageDraw import os def grid(pages,out,cols=6,w=330): ims=[(p,Image.open(f'pages/cole/p-{p:03d}.j
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/trees; python3 - <<'E'
from PIL import Image, ImageDraw
import os
def grid(pages,out,cols=6,w=330):
    ims=[(p,Image.open(f'pages/cole/p-{p:03d}.jpg')) for p in pages if os.path.exists(f'pages/cole/p-{p:03d}.jpg')]
    h=int(w*926/617); rows=(len(ims)+cols-1)//cols
    S=Image.new('L',(cols*w,rows*(h+14)),255);d=ImageDraw.Draw(S)
    for i,(p,im) in enumerate(ims):
        x=(i%cols)*w;y=(i//cols)*(h+14); S.paste(im.resize((w-4,h)),(x,y+14));d.text((x+4,y+1),str(p),fill=0)
    S.save(out,quality=85)
grid(list(range(196,208)),'tmp/g196.jpg')
E
ls pages/cole | wc -l

    S=Image.new('L',(cols*w,rows*(h+14)),255);d=ImageDraw.Draw(S)

        x=(i%cols)*w;y=(i//cols)*(h+14); S.paste(im.resize((w-4,h)),(x,y+14));d.text((x+4,y+1),str(p),fill=0)
    S.save(out,quality=85)

cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/trees; python3 - <<'E'
from PIL import Image, ImageDraw
import os
def grid(pages,out,cols=5,w=400):
    ims=[(p,Image.open(f'pages/halle/h-{p:03d}.jpg')) for p in pages if os.path.exists(f'pages/halle/h-{p:03d}.jpg')]
    h=int(w*782/559); rows=(len(ims)+cols-1)//cols
    S=Image.new('L',(cols*w,rows*(h+14)),255);d=ImageDraw.Draw(S)
    for i,(p,im) in enumerate(ims):
        x=(i%cols)*w;y=(i//cols)*(h+14); S.paste(im.convert('L').resize((w-4,h)),(x,y+14));d.text((x+4,y+1),str(p),fill=0)
    S.save(out,quality=85)
grid(range(50,60),'tmp/h50.jpg')

cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/trees/pages/halle; H="$HOME/work/pixelart-studies/library/purchased/Tropical trees and forests  an architectural analysis (Hallé, Francis, Hallé-Oldeman).pdf"; for p in 228 229 230 231 252 254 272 250 251; do nice pdftoppm -f $p -l $p -r 100 -gray -jpeg "$H" h 2>/dev/null; done; ls h-2* ; cd ../..; python3 - <<'E'
from PIL import Image, ImageDraw
def grid(pages,out,cols=3,w=620):
    ims=[(p,Image.open(f'pages/halle/h-{p:03d}.jpg')) for p in pages]
    hs=[int(w*im.size[1]/im.size[0]) for _,im in ims]; h=max(hs); rows=(len(ims)+cols-1)//cols
    S=Image.new('L',(cols*w,rows*(h+14)),255);d=ImageDraw.Draw(S)
    for i,(p,im) in enumerate(ims):
        x=(i%cols)*w;y=(i//cols)*(h+14); S.paste(im.convert('L').resize((w-4,int(w*im.size[1]/im.size[0]))),(x,y+14));d.text((x+4,y+1),str(p),fill=0)
    S.save(out,quality=85)
grid([228,229,230,231],'tmp/hR.jpg',cols=4,w=500); grid([250,251,252,254,272],'tmp/hF.jpg',cols=5,w=420)
E

# The model: Cole (1915) and Hallé–Oldeman–Tomlinson (1978), in their own terms

Two books, read whole and looked at plate by plate (Cole: all 412 scanned pages as contact sheets in
`pages/cole3/`, figures zoomed where they carry the teaching; Hallé: the full text in
`library/text/`, and the plates of the illustrated key, the model descriptions I use, reiteration
and the forest profiles, `pages/halle/`). Page numbers are the books' own printed pages.
"Fig." and "Illus." are the books' own numbering. Where I paraphrase I stay in their vocabulary;
where I add a reading of my own it is marked **[mine]**.

The two books are one model seen from two ends. Cole is a painter who went to the botanists because
"the result looks generally unlike a tree" when the mechanism is not understood (p. 147). Hallé is a
botanist who found that a small number of growth programs generate a species' whole form. Cole's
Part II is, unknowingly, a sketch of Hallé's architecture (bud arrangement → branch plan → outline);
Hallé has nothing to say about light, edge, value or distance, which is most of Cole's Part I.
So: **Hallé tells you what the tree is; Cole tells you what it looks like, and why.**

---

## Part A. Cole, *The Artistic Anatomy of Trees*

Cole's aim, stated in the preface and again at p. 45: construction is not wanted "for a display of
learning", but because a painter who knows the type can paint the individual with freedom and keep
"the feeling of greatness" while drawing piece by piece. Knowledge of structure "gives to our
drawing an almost unconscious variety of light and shade" (p. 110).

### A1. Seeing: masses, spaces, balance (Ch. II–III, pp. 47–68)

- **Balance of the individual** (p. 47, Figs. 1–5). A leaning or curving trunk still carries its
  foliage over its base. On slopes trunks lean slightly *uphill* ("rake to aft" of a mast, Fig. 4);
  downhill lean looks wrong. Overhanging trees look balanced because their heavy boughs rise from
  the upper side. Exposed roots are "a stand"; a bole buried flush looks "shoved in like the
  flowersticks in our garden" (p. 49). The lines that tie bole to ground — roots, contact shadows,
  sharp-edged ground light between trunks — carry poise.
- **Single tree vs. mass** (pp. 49–51). A lone tree is read as pattern and line (stem traced to "a
  haze of twigs at the apex"); the isolated thing is focused more sharply and expected to be more
  exact. **Massed trees: one depth of colour; tones taken from one end or the middle of the scale,
  never both ends (Fig. 9)**; "some severity in the outline — a gimped edge (Fig. 10) is not conducive
  to grandeur". Massed trees seen from a height "look spotty and detached": paint them in long
  shadow, contre-jour, cloud shadow, or shafts of light, when detail is lost in flat tone.
- **Nature as spaces of light and dark** (p. 56, Figs. 14–24). A tree and its cast shadow are *one*
  dark shape (Figs. 14–15). Two trees of like tone are one form (16–17). Under uniform light, trees,
  ground and figure merge and "it is the space of grey between them that attracts attention" (18).
  Reflection doubles a dark (19). **Even spacing is to be avoided (Figs. 20–21, 25–26)**; exactly
  equal dark and light is too formal, total imbalance worse.
- **Draw the spaces first** (p. 59, Figs. 27–28, Illus. 11). For a row of trunks, draw the sky and
  ground spaces between them, then redraw the trunks left behind. A pine wood in flat light is "as
  wearisome as the bare hop poles of Kent"; low sun spaces the stems into lit, grey and dark.
- **Scale** (pp. 59–63). Equal small trees in open country look like figures on a stage (Fig. 29).
  Cut trees off at the frame (Fig. 31). A sapling beside a trunk gives the trunk its girth (Fig. 34).
  A bare larch stem "merely seems terribly tall"; an ash's branches divide it into measurable lengths.
- **Masses and delicacy** (pp. 63–64, Illus. 13–14). Decided masses against indefinite ones; a
  thinly clad tree in front of a massed one either contrasts sharply or blurs its edge into sky; "a
  great space of indefinite form can be balanced by a small distinct one".
- **The distance ladder** (pp. 64–68; Figs. 35–39). This is the single most important passage for us.
  - *Far:* "rendered flat in tone by the atmosphere, … recognised by the pattern it makes against the
    sky … an oblong, a semicircle, a cone". Elm = border of straight lines; beech = "undecided edges
    of loose sprays"; Lombardy poplar a sentinel among squat oaks; **"There is no edge to the Larch
    wood — just a haze; … the upright lines of the trunks show as faint streaks of grey"**. Skyline
    groups are "architectural"; woods fix the contours of hills; plantations step down behind a hill.
    Not "a number of monotonous dots which serve no purpose".
  - *Middle:* local colour shows through the air (ash lighter than sycamore, elm, oak; pinewoods "a
    startling patch of dark"); and **species now show inside the tree as well as at its edge: "The
    shadows between the foliage may alone betray the species; in some they are sharp and dark, in
    others faint and confused, massed or detached, forming lines consistently in one direction or
    another."** Beech: thin layers and detached spiked sprays; lime: heavier layers, drooping curves;
    oak: tufted, star-shaped projections; sycamore/plane: massed sharp-edged; poplar: dotted leaves;
    willow: blurred edges. Direction of the chief branches and the junctions can be followed.
  - *Near:* individual traits, wind, the treatment of twigs and leaves against the sky; "a single leaf
    may look as large as a distant tree".

### A2. The tree against the sky (Ch. IV, pp. 69–81)

The edge (pp. 69–71, Illus. 18): "no part of it is quite sharply defined, and there is a great
variety in the intensity of definitiveness." Reasons: foreshortened leaves at the margin, light
passing on either side, far-side tips seen through, stalks invisible. Lowest hanging leaves are
"much more distinct, and usually darker".

The rules for sky holes, which he states as a list (p. 71):

1. **The extreme margins of holes in foliage are diffused and colourless** — "not sharp, dark, or
   bright coloured".
2. **Small sky spaces appear as circular lights with diffused edges, the strongest light in the
   centre** ("globes").
3. **Holes veiled by small leaves and twigs are darker than the open sky.**
4. **Local colour is strongest in the centre of the largest masses**; the edge is less coloured than
   the bulk.
5. **The upper or lower edge of a foliage group is sharp or indistinct according to which projects
   in front** — "it explains the formation of the branches".
6. Small leaves (elm) vs. large (sycamore), narrow (willow) vs. broad (alder), spaced (black poplar)
   vs. close (oak) make completely different edges (Illus. 19–26).

Leafless trees (p. 78): a **border haze of little twigs "scarcely differing from the colour of the
sky"**, inside it a closer network that veils, and against the veil "the exquisite lines of the
larger boughs can be clearly traced". Winter holes are irregular, bounded by boughs.

Patterns of holes (pp. 78–80, Figs. 40–41): shaped by the habit of the species; **"construct these
patterns clearly, in fact to draw the mass of the tree by them"** (Corot places them on a preconceived
pattern). Holes are linked into chains — by small lights, or by "a strong edge, where massed foliage
ends at the light", carried to the next — so the mass is not "cut up into meaningless sections".
The opposite also has its place: in a half-grown ash, "grey or dark spots of unequal size, but of
equal value interspersed with the sky" (Illus. 27). Clausen's warning: trees "all cut out at the
edges, like trees on a stage"; see the tree as "a great dome, spreading up and rounding into the sky,
with the light showing on it and through it".

### A3. The outline (Ch. V, pp. 82–94)

Outline is not "a scallop" (Figs. 43–44). The map-of-England lesson (Figs. 45–46): draw the chief
curves and straight lines, not every inlet. Seven laws (pp. 82–83):

1. Chief lines are carried across breaks, picked up by one form after another (Figs. 47–48); the
   composing line can run along a *shadow* edge instead of the outline (Fig. 49).
2. There is a choice of chief line, and it changes with the hour (Figs. 50–55).
3. Insist on the line's distinguishing feature; don't let accidents interrupt it (Figs. 56–59):
   "how easy it is to miss just that straight bit that makes the curve look so buxom".
4. A line's value comes from contrast with another, and from repetition nearby.
5. Similar lines near one another beat dissimilar ones (the beech's recurved lower branches).
6. Lines weary without slight variation — "a qualified monotony"; ruled parallels are dreary.
7. **Straight lines = restraint, dignity, strength; slight curves = grace; circles = weight and
   "a coarse exuberance".** "The outline of a tree is made up of many straight lines. … **the
   comparative length of these straight pieces determine the character of the curve**" (p. 92,
   Figs. 62–64). Elms are "outlined by straight-cut edges"; three or four straight lines cut the
   edges of all the main blocks (Figs. 65–66). A young fir's "oft repeated breaks" are displeasing,
   "far more so when the dentated edge is emphasized by a harsh outline" (Fig. 53).

### A4. Lines of the branches (Ch. VI, pp. 95–109)

- **Each species keeps one type of line**: small angle, large angle, slight curve, full curve, or
  elbow; "every twig is formed on the plan of its neighbour, and every bough is but a twig grown old"
  (pp. 99–100, Illus. 34–36: white poplar vs. blackthorn). Unity from the recurring type; variety
  from viewpoint, foreshortening, age, accident (Fig. 72).
- Mixing types is "complex and discordant" (Fig. 74); lines in one direction act as one line
  (Fig. 73, young oak); the matches exercise (Figs. 76–79): parallel but unequally spaced good;
  crossing anyhow bad; equal length and equidistant "impossible".
- Stems: a vertical start breaking into a short curve then a longer reversed one (Figs. 83–85); the
  accent at a junction makes two curves out of one; one outline of a bough runs continuous past a
  junction while the other is broken (Fig. 86). Birch: the long curve made of lesser ones — beginners
  make "a lumpy wriggle reminiscent of bent wire" (Figs. 81–82).
- **Pendent branches**: from the upper side, down, then up — "all the suggestion of a steel spring"
  (ash, Fig. 87, Illus. 39); in apple, oak, alder, thorn the same formation "becomes a firm elbow or
  crozier". Birch branches "loose straying" vs. the firm lines of ash (Illus. 40).

### A5. Light and shade on structure (Ch. VII, pp. 110–120)

- **Planes and a tone scale** (pp. 110–114, Figs. 91–92): "Each surface … would represent to our
  minds a definite tone reserved for it and for all other surfaces that were in a similar plane." A
  wide hollow takes four tones, a sharp furrow two. Twigs are not gaspipes but flat-sided, keeled
  (Figs. 93–94); junctions give flat lit spaces.
- **The clipped-yew exercise** (p. 114, Fig. 95): "Under the simple light of a grey sky the foliage
  of a tree can be considered and studied as the planes that construct a great dome"; one tone per
  plane; over-reaching forms catch a light not of their plane.
- **Sun is a second set of tones** (pp. 114–115): glitter and sunlit sprays "are an accident to be
  added to the bulk of the tree … otherwise we get all sparkle but no tree to hold it. … think of our
  tree as made up of a certain set of tones, and for the sunlit parts to use a new and lighter set,
  quite apart from the others."
- **Contre-jour** (p. 115): dark trunk and boughs against bright thin far-side leaves "of two or three
  tones according to their density"; near projecting groups dark and greyer from sky reflection;
  "rich green in the recesses" from leaf-to-leaf bounce; tops and edges lighter grey "as they recede
  round its dome". High sun: glitter at the apex and the low jutting ends. Branches crossing a bright
  gap "lose their edges and darkness and modelling, and become thin strips of undecided half tone"
  (also p. 45: "slender forms seen against the light appear more slender").
- **Bark varies the cylinder** (p. 115): smooth holly takes only its strip of light; fissured bark
  carries darks into the light and lights into the shadow; reflected light under horizontal boughs;
  cast shadows of small branches at junctions; give-and-take of edges against dark and light grounds.
- **Twig number** (p. 116): the twigs of a larch or birch "collectively would equal the girth of the
  parent boughs"; larch twigs "disappear in a haze while the Chestnut shoots stand detached".
- **Colour** (pp. 116–119): distance assimilates to sky; "each colour has its own grey"; full colour
  is usually *absent* where the strongest light falls (the lit parts reflect the sky), and colour is
  often strongest in shadowed hollows reinforced by reflection.

### A6. Situation, age, weather (Ch. VIII, pp. 121–132)

- Crowded trees: long clean trunk, weak lower boughs; free-grown: divides low, spreads; exposed:
  squat or straggling, or "shorn close by the wind like over-grown bushes"; crowded on one side,
  fills the other; road trees arch.
- **Age** (pp. 123–128): Scots/stone pine cone → "umbrella" (it cannot form new boughs below old
  ones, so tiers die upward leaving a bare trunk with stumps and nodes); larch keeps only the upper
  cone on "an immense bare trunk". **In well-grown trees each main bough ends in a straight line and
  these build the outline, with small sky spaces between them; later the spaces widen, gaps appear,
  symmetry is lost and "irregular or grotesque forms" come.** Young trees make long loose sprays;
  old ones "the closeness of multiplied forms".
- Wind (p. 128): species respond differently (birch bends, oak and alder defy, larch twigs swing in
  unison); pale undersides give each species its windy-day grey; coast trees are "transfixed in the
  act of movement" (p. 134).
- Snow and hoar frost (pp. 129–130); moonlight (pp. 130–132): level and vertical merge in one shadow;
  trees distinguished only "by the loss or comparative sharpness of the outline" — birch a haze,
  oak and elm "blank spaces of dark with straight-cut edges".

### A7. Recognition by outline (Ch. X–XI, pp. 147–159)

- We read things by outline and by the *texture of the outline* (thatch: indistinct edge; tile:
  sharp and dark). The cow is a straight back and a triangle; the man a knob and a lozenge (Figs.
  97–100): the painter indoors adds legs and faces that were never visible.
- **Outline follows from branch lengths** (pp. 151–153, Figs. 101–107): the leader grows faster than
  the laterals, so a young tree is a *cone* not a pyramid; if the lowest boughs keep growing, a cone
  or dome; if the middle ones outstrip, a circle (horse chestnut); if the upper, a pear (hornbeam,
  stone pine). **Each side branch is a reduced replica of the whole, but inclined: its lower
  branchlets lie in one flat plane, its tip spreads in many directions (Figs. 108–110). The gaps
  between branch extremities are "the second great characteristic of young trees"; with age they
  fill in** (Illus. 61).
- **Branch angle makes the species** (pp. 155–158): Lombardy poplar's acute angle confines all in a
  narrow cone, branches advancing "by steps" as outer branchlets take over (Fig. 111); black poplar,
  same leaves, wide angle, free-growing; oak: right angles, crowded, sturdy horizontal boughs, no
  underside branches (Fig. 113); ash: paired, spaced, upswept boughs with pendent branches; elm: few
  limbs dividing rapidly into many small twigs, so "each characteristic straight line of the outline
  is the termination of one bough"; beech: the twig's zigzag repeated in flat layers (Fig. 115).

### A8. Buds make branches (Ch. XII–XIV, pp. 160–198)

- A bud is a compressed shoot; each species follows one of six or seven plans (p. 160): **opposite
  decussate** (ash, maple, sycamore, horse chestnut, elder); **alternate two-ranked** (beech, elm,
  hazel, lime, hornbeam: "bud and shoot lie in one plane … all the boughs would be in flat layers
  after the manner of a fern", zigzag shoots, p. 176, Fig. 136); **three-ranked** (alder: boughs
  "evenly distributed without much interlacing", identified by angle, p. 181); **five or more ranks**
  (oak, willows, poplars, cherry: "the want of order, rather than its observance, becomes the
  feature"); **clustered/whorled** (spruce, Scots pine, silver fir, holly: branches radiate "like the
  spokes of an umbrella"; on a horizontal bough the underside twigs curve up and the upper-side ones
  grow outward, p. 183, Fig. 143).
- **Failure of buds** (pp. 172–176, Figs. 130–135): tip lost → forks (Y); tip and one side lost → an
  angle; one side bud as leader → a curve; habitual substitution makes the ash's "gentle curves", the
  guelder rose's "frame full of abrupt angles".
- Additional buds (pp. 183–185): pines cannot bud from old wood; willows, elms, alders, hollies sprout
  (adventitious shoots on alder boles, Illus. 82; vertical shoots on fallen holly). Arrested twigs:
  spurs and long shoots (thorn, larch, pine). **Old trees: short much-forked twigs instead of long
  shoots; lateral spread; lower boughs decay** (p. 186).
- **How a tree is built up** (Ch. XIV, pp. 187–198): leader continued from the tip = "a telescope
  drawn out" (Fig. 144); from a side bud = repeated curves or alternating breaks (145–146); forks
  (147). Junctions soften with girth; in a fork one limb predominates (150). **The trunk is of equal
  thickness between boughs and steps down at each ("after the fashion of a telescope", Fig. 155);
  spruce and larch seem to taper only because their scars are close (157–159); the two limbs of a
  fork together measure rather more than the trunk below (Fig. 160).** A few conspicuous limbs that
  "suddenly ramify" (elm) look utterly different from a gradual succession (ash) (Fig. 161).
  **Pendent boughs grow "a succession of curved steps": weighted shoots bend, new shoots rise from
  their upper side and bend in turn; inner branchlets die for lack of light** (pp. 194–198, Fig. 162,
  Illus. 90–91).

### A9. Details (Ch. XV–XXI, pp. 201–318)

Leaves follow buds (p. 208): most leaves lie horizontal or slightly drooping; opposite pairs set at
right angles need vertical shoots (stalks lengthen below, Figs. 163–166); alternate two-ranked leaves
lie in one plane with their twigs "as if pressed under a weight" (beech, hornbeam, Figs. 170–171);
spirals point toward the twig tip; conifer needles bend round the twig into one plane on horizontal
shoots (yew); clustered = oak's star bunches, larch and pine shuttlecocks (Illus. 104). Young leaves
hang limp (horse chestnut "a damp half-closed umbrella") and set the seasonal key. Texture (glossy,
hairy, granular) decides how much sky a leaf reflects (p. 233). Leaf plans (Figs. 200–250). Flowers
and catkins change a tree's whole appearance (alder, hazel, birch catkins; p. 207, Illus. 131).
Bark (pp. 311–318): three methods of growth → smooth (beech), scaly (plane, birch), furrowed (oak);
twig colours grade into the greys of the old branches — "the harmony of the woods in winter".

---

## Part B. Hallé, Oldeman & Tomlinson, *Tropical Trees and Forests: An Architectural Analysis*

### B1. Architecture and model (pp. 74–81)

- **Architecture** = "the visible, morphological expression of the genetic blueprint of a tree at
  any one time"; **model** = "the growth program which determines the successive architectural
  phases" — visible only as *a series* of architectures (p. 75). Not shape, not habit; size-free:
  a 10-cm herb and a 50-m tree can share a model (p. 76).
- The models are named points in an **architectural continuum**, "centers of population on a
  demographic map" (p. 77). 23 models (key pp. 84–97).
- **Criteria** (pp. 80–81): (1) *life-span of meristems* — monopodial vs. sympodial (the most
  important); (2) *differentiation of meristems*: vegetative vs. sexual (terminal vs. lateral
  flowering), **orthotropy** (erect, radial, spiral/decussate) vs. **plagiotropy** (horizontal,
  dorsiventral, distichous), and *mixed* axes that change from one to the other; (3) **rhythmic**
  growth (tiers; short internodes + scale leaves alternating with long internodes) vs. continuous;
  (4) chronology of branching.
- **Corner's rules** (p. 83): axial conformity — the stouter the axis, the larger its appendages;
  diminution on ramification — the more branching, the smaller branches and leaves. Leonardo: all
  branches at a level equal the trunk (Stevens: in fact slightly *exceed* — Cole's Fig. 160 agrees).
- Visible branch orders are few: about 5–6 (Acer rubrum 5; Eucalyptus 7; Taxus 9 the upper limit);
  more in small-leaved species (p. 41).
- Secondary dorsiventrality (p. 39): horizontal shoots of spiral conifers turn their needles into
  one plane (Abies, Picea), with anisophylly in Tsuga.

### B2. The models that matter for Washington

(Definitions quoted from the text; illustrations: key pp. 92–97.)

| model | definition (their words, shortened) | temperate / PNW members |
|---|---|---|
| **Massart** (p. 191) | "orthotropic, monopodial trunk with rhythmic growth … regular tiers of branches"; branches **plagiotropic**, "become pendulous with age" | "a number of temperate conifers ('Christmas trees') like species of Abies, Picea", Taxus; Araucaria the type; **balsam fir krummholz at timberline: orthotropic shoots lost to wind, "plagiotropy is fixed and the branches continue to spread horizontally close to the ground"** (p. 199). Late self-pruning + adventitious branches form "nesting crowns" (p. 193). |
| **Rauh** (p. 221) | "monopodial trunk which grows rhythmically and so develops tiers of branches, the branches themselves morphogenetically identical with the trunk" (all orthotropic); flowers lateral | Pinus (most), **Quercus** ("probably most species"), **Acer**, Fraxinus. "Supple": any meristem replaces the leader; "almost impossible to find an individual of ash, oak, or maple which conforms precisely"; red oak "never has a straight trunk" (p. 226). |
| **Troll** (p. 242) | "Axes are all plagiotropic, the architecture being built by their continual superposition"; each axis part trunk, part branch, the base erected secondarily (after leaf fall) | Ulmus, Fagus; the commonest model (20–30% of trees); plastic — dome in the open, cone in the forest (p. 249). **Tsuga canadensis: "drooping leader" secondarily erected by compression wood; "strongly suggests … Troll's model"** (p. 246); elsewhere "hemlock may show Mangenot's model" (p. 329). |
| **Scarrone** (p. 213) | rhythmic orthotropic trunk; branches orthotropic, **sympodial through terminal inflorescences** | mango; candelabra crowns. **[mine]** a fit for Pacific madrone (terminal panicles, sympodial). |
| **Leeuwenberg** (p. 145) | sympodial throughout, each module ending in an inflorescence, 2+ branches below it | castor bean, dragon tree. **[mine]** madrone's periphery tends here. |
| **Attims** (p. 228) | as Rauh but continuous growth | uncommon. |
| **Mangenot / Champagnat** (pp. 233, 238) | mixed axes: erect then horizontal / erect then bending by weight | hemlock possibly Mangenot. |

The book does not treat Pseudotsuga, Thuja, Picea sitchensis, Larix, Alnus or Arbutus directly. My
assignments, with reasons, are in LESSONS.md and in `src/species_models.py` — all **[mine]**.

### B3. Reiteration (Ch. 4, pp. 269–284)

- **Reiteration** = "the repetition of overall plant growth patterns starting from meristems";
  a latent meristem makes a second copy of the model on the first (p. 272). Evidence: **bayonet
  joints** (a substituted trunk, p. 270), forks, stump and root suckers, the **river-bank effect** (a
  leaning trunk over water or a gap throws up vertical axes decreasing in size distally).
- *Sequential* branching (the model's own) vs. *reiteration* (delayed, irregular, unpredictable)
  (p. 273). Triggered by **surplus energy**: when the model's meristems run at capacity, extra light
  goes into new trunks (p. 278).
- **The forest tree** (Fig. 74, pp. 275–280): initial model in the undergrowth, then waves of
  reiteration, each wave "more numerous and smaller" — **arborescent** (the big limbs), **frutescent**
  ("shrubs" on them), **subfrutescent**, **herbaceous** (a "field of weeds" in the crown top without
  roots) — and at the herbaceous stage the tree has reached maximum size. The crown top becomes a
  flat dome of tiny complexes on a few big forked limbs.
- **The free-standing tree** (Fig. 75, pp. 280–284): reiteration starts early; the crown grows wider
  than tall, hemispheric, short trunk; the periphery and lower crown become **dorsiventral**
  (plagiotropic, one-sided, "river-bank effect"), the centre keeps ascending axes, "a microcosm of the
  whole forest canopy". Forest trees "zigzag to higher levels" through successive gaps.
- **Trees of the future / present / past** (pp. 320–325): future = model-conforming, suppressed,
  slender (can exceed H = 100·D in "ecological chimneys"); present = fully reiterated, stable;
  past = broken, a "cluster of rather humble reiterated complexes at or below the level where the
  trunk is broken", "architectural chaos", H ≪ 100·D.
- **Morphological inversion point** (pp. 325–327): the lowest living major branch or first reiterated
  fork; below it one big unit (the trunk), above it smaller and smaller units. **Crown depth / total
  height > ½ → free-standing; ≈ ½ → vigorous forest tree; < ½ → suppressed or senescent.**
- **Gymnosperms** (pp. 327–330): "model conformity is much stronger than reiteration"; sequential
  branch complexes of decreasing size do the job reiteration does in broadleaves, within strict
  symmetry; hemlock's higher-order branches are dorsiventral, almost like a compound leaf; hemlocks
  grow slowly and steadily under hardwoods and finally overtop them (Fig. 93, Harvard Pond profile).

### B4. The forest (Ch. 5, pp. 332–386)

- Forest plots are drawn as profiles (width ⅓–⅔ of forest height, length ≥ height) with sets of the
  present (outlined), future (stippled), past (p. 333ff.; Fig. 93).
- The **morphological inversion surface** joins the inversion points of the canopy trees: nearly
  horizontal above half-height in old well-structured forest, **wavy** in growing or disturbed forest
  (Fig. 104). The ecological inversion surface touches the ground at every gap.
- **Imbrication on slopes** (pp. 360–364, Figs. 106–107A): canopies overlap downhill "like roofing
  tiles"; crowns at the downhill edge of each tile are asymmetric toward the light; above ~25–35°
  continuous turnover. **Riverside** (Fig. 107D): the whole canopy is built of asymmetric crowns
  curving down to the water or road. **Chablis**: the fall of a tree and the gap, debris and damaged
  survivors it leaves (p. 368).

---

## Part C. Where the two meet (the joint model I work from) **[mine, from both]**

1. **Growth program → outline.** Hallé's criteria (orthotropic/plagiotropic, rhythmic/continuous,
   monopodial/sympodial) are the causes of Cole's outlines (cone vs. dome, gaps between branch
   extremities, flat layers, straight-cut edges, pendulous steps).
2. **Age is a program, not a scale factor.** Cole's cone → umbrella and "multiplied forms" of old
   age; Hallé's model → waves of reiteration → herbaceous crown; the inversion point rising.
3. **Every bough is a twig grown old** (Cole) = **reiteration repeats the model at smaller scale**
   (Hallé) = one type of line per species.
4. **Situation decides the shape the program takes**: crowded vs. free (Cole Ch. VIII) = forest vs.
   free-standing tree and the river-bank effect (Hallé Figs. 74–75).
5. **The painter's layer on top**: distance ladder, edge/hole rules, planes of a dome, sun as a
   second tone set, masses from one end of the scale — Cole only.

Write: books/trees/src/arch.py
"""Hallé–Oldeman architectural models as growth programs.

A tree is grown year by year from its apical meristems (Hallé ch. 2-3). Each axis has:

  - orthotropy (erect, radial) or plagiotropy (horizontal, dorsiventral) -- Hallé p. 81
  - rhythmic growth: one growth unit (GU) a year ending in a tier of laterals (Massart, Rauh)
  - monopodial (the apex lives on) or sympodial growth (the apex ends -- in a flower or
    by abortion -- and 1-3 laterals below it continue: Scarrone, Leeuwenberg; Cole's
    "failure of buds", figs. 130-135)
  - a lateral growth program: GUs shrink with the lateral's age (Cole p. 151: "branches have
    a definite distance to grow"), so the oldest -- lowest -- boughs are longest: the cone.
Then the opportunistic layer (Hallé ch. 4): self-pruning below the light, leader loss and
substitution ("bayonet joints"), and reiteration (a branch dedifferentiates into a new copy
of the model) once the tree has surplus vigour.

Units: metres, years. Output: Tree with segments (p0, p1, radius, order, kind) and foliage
clusters (position, size, normal, plagiotropic?). Radii by the pipe rule with exponent 2.4
(Cole fig. 160: the limbs of a fork together are "rather more" than the trunk below;
Hallé p. 83, Leonardo/Stevens).
"""
import numpy as np
from dataclasses import dataclass, field

UP = np.array([0.0, 0.0, 1.0])


def _norm(v):
    n = np.linalg.norm(v)
    return v / n if n > 1e-9 else v


def _perp(d):
    a = np.array([1.0, 0, 0]) if abs(d[0]) < 0.9 else np.array([0, 1.0, 0])
    u = _norm(np.cross(d, a))
    return u, np.cross(d, u)


@dataclass
class Species:
    name: str
    model: str = "massart"        # massart | rauh | troll | scarrone
    height: float = 60.0          # asymptotic height (m)
    h_rate: float = 0.9           # leader GU length when young (m/yr)
    h_tau: float = 90.0           # height approach time (yr)
    tier: tuple = (3, 5)          # laterals per tier (whorl) on the trunk
    inter: int = 0                # extra scattered laterals per GU (interwhorl branches)
    angle: float = 80.0           # insertion angle of trunk laterals (deg from vertical)
    angle_top: float = 55.0       # ... near the leader
    lat_rate: float = 0.55        # lateral GU length / leader GU length when young
    lat_tau: float = 18.0         # lateral GU decay time (yr): sets the bough's final length
    plagio: bool = True           # trunk laterals plagiotropic (Massart) or orthotropic (Rauh)
    sag: float = 0.010            # gravity bending per metre of bough per year (rad)
    tip_up: float = 0.0           # upturn of the bough tip (cedar J, spruce), rad per m near tip
    sub_n: int = 2                # laterals per GU on branches (order >=1)
    sub_angle: float = 55.0
    sub_rate: float = 0.45        # sub-branch GU / parent GU
    pendulous: float = 0.0        # 0..1: sub-branches hang (spruce curtains, cedar fans)
    max_order: int = 3
    shade_prune: float = 0.35     # crown base (fraction of height) reached in a closed forest
    open_prune: float = 0.05      # ... in the open
    stub_keep: float = 0.4        # chance a pruned bough leaves a dead stub
    leader_loss: float = 0.0      # yearly chance the leader is lost (Rauh "supple")
    sinuous: float = 0.0          # random wander of orthotropic axes (rad/yr)
    nod: float = 0.0              # leader nods (Troll/Mangenot hemlock): droop of current GU (rad)
    sympodial_age: int = 999      # from this age, apices end each year and fork (Scarrone)
    fork_n: tuple = (2, 3)
    reiter_age: int = 999         # from this age, reiteration can start
    reiter_rate: float = 0.0      # yearly chance per vigorous upper bough to turn orthotropic
    reiter_max: int = 12
    top_break: float = 0.0        # yearly chance of losing the top after reiter_age (old growth)
    needle_years: int = 5         # years of foliage kept on an axis
    cluster: float = 0.8          # foliage cluster radius (m) at order 1
    pad: bool = True              # dorsiventral pads (plagiotropic sprays) vs balls
    trunk_k: float = 0.012        # trunk radius per metre of height (pipe base)
    flare: float = 0.0            # root flare multiple


@dataclass
class Axis:
    pts: list                     # list of np points (node positions)
    order: int
    kind: str                     # 'ortho' | 'plagio'
    born: int
    alive: bool = True
    apex: bool = True
    parent: int = -1
    at: int = 0                   # index of parent node
    age_nodes: list = field(default_factory=list)   # year each node was made
    lat_age: int = 0              # years this lateral has grown
    dead: bool = False
    reiterated: bool = False


class Tree:
    def __init__(self, sp: Species, seed=0, open_grown=False):
        self.sp, self.rng, self.open = sp, np.random.default_rng(seed), open_grown
        self.axes = [Axis([np.zeros(3)], 0, 'ortho', 0, age_nodes=[0])]
        self.year = 0
        self.dir = {0: UP.copy()}
        self.nreiter = 0

    # ---- growth -------------------------------------------------------------------
    def leader_gu(self, y):
        sp = self.sp
        # height ~ H (1 - exp(-t/tau)) with initial rate h_rate; GU = derivative
        return max(0.02, sp.h_rate * np.exp(-y / sp.h_tau) if sp.h_rate * sp.h_tau >= sp.height
                   else sp.height / sp.h_tau * np.exp(-y / sp.h_tau))

    def height(self):
        return max(p[2] for a in self.axes if a.order == 0 or a.reiterated for p in a.pts)

    def grow(self, years):
        for _ in range(years):
            self.year += 1
            self._year()
        return self

    def _year(self):
        sp, rng, y = self.sp, self.rng, self.year
        lead = self.leader_gu(y)
        new = []
        for i, a in enumerate(self.axes):
            if not a.alive or not a.apex:
                continue
            d = self.dir[i]
            if a.order == 0 or a.reiterated:
                L = lead * (1.0 if a.order == 0 else 0.8)
            else:
                L = lead * sp.lat_rate * (sp.sub_rate ** (a.order - 1)) * np.exp(-a.lat_age / sp.lat_tau)
                if L < 0.015:
                    a.apex = False
                    continue
            # direction update
            if a.kind == 'ortho':
                d = _norm(d + 0.35 * UP + sp.sinuous * rng.normal(size=3) * np.array([1, 1, 0]))
            else:
                # plagiotropic: keeps near horizontal, bends by load
                hz = _norm(d * np.array([1, 1, 0])) if np.linalg.norm(d[:2]) > 1e-6 else d
                d = _norm(0.8 * d + 0.2 * hz)
            p0 = a.pts[-1]
            # rhythmic GU: 3 sub-nodes so bending shows as a curve
            nseg = 3 if L > 0.3 else 1
            for k in range(nseg):
                a.pts.append(p0 + d * L * (k + 1) / nseg)
                a.age_nodes.append(y)
            self.dir[i] = d
            if a.order > 0 and not a.reiterated:
                a.lat_age += 1
            # --- branching at the end of the GU (the tier) ---
            node = len(a.pts) - 1
            if a.order == 0 or a.reiterated:
                n = rng.integers(sp.tier[0], sp.tier[1] + 1)
                h = a.pts[-1][2] / max(1.0, sp.height)
                ang = np.radians(sp.angle + (sp.angle_top - sp.angle) * min(1, h) * 0) if y < 3 else np.radians(sp.angle)
                az0 = rng.uniform(0, 2 * np.pi)
                kids = [(np.radians(sp.angle_top) if y < 4 else ang, az0 + 2 * np.pi * j / n + rng.normal(0, 0.25), node) for j in range(n)]
                for j in range(sp.inter):  # interwhorl laterals along the GU
                    kids.append((ang, rng.uniform(0, 2 * np.pi), max(1, node - rng.integers(1, nseg + 1))))
                for (an, az, nd) in kids:
                    u, v = _perp(d)
                    kd = _norm(np.cos(an) * d + np.sin(an) * (np.cos(az) * u + np.sin(az) * v))
                    new.append((Axis([a.pts[nd].copy()], a.order + 1, 'plagio' if sp.plagio else 'ortho', y,
                                     parent=i, at=nd, age_nodes=[y]), kd))
            elif a.order < sp.max_order:
                # sub-branches: plagiotropic parents branch in their own plane (dorsiventral)
                for s in (-1, 1)[:sp.sub_n] if sp.sub_n <= 2 else range(sp.sub_n):
                    side = s if sp.sub_n <= 2 else rng.choice([-1, 1])
                    hz = _norm(np.cross(d, UP)) if a.kind == 'plagio' else _perp(d)[0]
                    an = np.radians(sp.sub_angle)
                    kd = _norm(np.cos(an) * d + np.sin(an) * side * hz)
                    if sp.pendulous > 0 and a.kind == 'plagio':
                        kd = _norm(kd - sp.pendulous * 1.2 * UP)
                    new.append((Axis([a.pts[node].copy()], a.order + 1, a.kind, y, parent=i, at=node,
                                     age_nodes=[y]), kd))
            # --- sympodial ending (Scarrone / Leeuwenberg / Cole's forks) ---
            if y >= sp.sympodial_age and (a.order > 0 or y > sp.sympodial_age + 2):
                a.apex = False
                nf = rng.integers(sp.fork_n[0], sp.fork_n[1] + 1)
                for j in range(nf):
                    u, v = _perp(d)
                    az = rng.uniform(0, 2 * np.pi)
                    kd = _norm(np.cos(0.6) * d + np.sin(0.6) * (np.cos(az) * u + np.sin(az) * v))
                    ax = Axis([a.pts[-1].copy()], a.order, a.kind, y, parent=i, at=len(a.pts) - 1, age_nodes=[y])
                    ax.lat_age, ax.reiterated = a.lat_age, a.reiterated
                    new.append((ax, kd))
        for ax, kd in new:
            self.axes.append(ax)
            self.dir[len(self.axes) - 1] = kd
        self._bend()
        self._opportunistic()

    def _bend(self):
        """Gravity: plagiotropic boughs sag with their length and age; tips may turn up."""
        sp = self.sp
        for i, a in enumerate(self.axes):
            if a.kind != 'plagio' or len(a.pts) < 2 or a.dead:
                continue
            P = np.array(a.pts)
            seg = np.diff(P, axis=0)
            Ls = np.linalg.norm(seg, axis=1)
            tot = Ls.sum()
            s = np.cumsum(Ls)
            # rotate each segment downward by sag * (load outboard of it)
            load = tot - s + Ls
            new = [P[0]]
            for k in range(len(seg)):
                v = seg[k]
                drop = sp.sag * load[k] * (1 + 0.5 * a.order)
                if sp.tip_up and s[k] > 0.7 * tot:
                    drop -= sp.tip_up * (s[k] - 0.7 * tot)
                h = np.linalg.norm(v[:2])
                if h > 1e-9:
                    ang = np.arctan2(v[2], h) - drop * 0.1
                    ang = np.clip(ang, -1.45, 1.2)
                    hv = v[:2] / h
                    L = np.linalg.norm(v)
                    v = np.array([hv[0] * np.cos(ang) * L, hv[1] * np.cos(ang) * L, np.sin(ang) * L])
                new.append(new[-1] + v)
            a.pts = list(new)
            if a.apex:
                self.dir[i] = _norm(new[-1] - new[-2])
        # children follow their parent's moved node
        for i, a in enumerate(self.axes):
            if a.parent >= 0 and a.pts:
                p = self.axes[a.parent].pts
                if a.at < len(p):
                    off = p[a.at] - a.pts[0]
                    if np.any(off):
                        a.pts = [q + off for q in a.pts]

    def _opportunistic(self):
        sp, rng, y = self.sp, self.rng, self.year
        H = self.height()
        # self-pruning: boughs below the crown base die (Hallé: the inversion point rises)
        frac = sp.open_prune if self.open else sp.shade_prune * min(1, y / 60.0)
        base = H * frac
        for a in self.axes:
            if a.order == 1 and not a.dead and a.pts[0][2] < base and not a.reiterated:
                a.dead, a.alive = True, False
                if rng.random() < sp.stub_keep:
                    # stub: keep the first metre or so
                    keep = [a.pts[0]]
                    for q in a.pts[1:]:
                        if np.linalg.norm(q - a.pts[0]) > rng.uniform(0.3, 1.5):
                            break
                        keep.append(q)
                    a.pts = keep if len(keep) > 1 else a.pts[:2]
                else:
                    a.pts = a.pts[:1]
        for a in self.axes:  # descendants of dead axes die
            if a.parent >= 0 and self.axes[a.parent].dead and not a.dead:
                a.dead, a.alive, a.pts = True, False, a.pts[:1]
        # leader loss -> substitution by the uppermost lateral (bayonet joint)
        lead = [i for i, a in enumerate(self.axes) if a.order == 0 and a.apex]
        if lead and sp.leader_loss and rng.random() < sp.leader_loss:
            i = lead[0]
            self.axes[i].apex = False
            kids = [j for j, a in enumerate(self.axes) if a.parent == i and a.alive and not a.dead]
            if kids:
                j = max(kids, key=lambda j: self.axes[j].pts[0][2] + 0.01 * j)
                k = self.axes[j]
                k.order, k.kind, k.lat_age, k.reiterated = 0, 'ortho', 0, False
                self.dir[j] = _norm(self.dir[j] + 1.5 * UP)
        # old growth: loss of the top
        if y > sp.reiter_age and sp.top_break and rng.random() < sp.top_break:
            tops = [a for a in self.axes if (a.order == 0 or a.reiterated) and a.apex]
            if tops:
                t = max(tops, key=lambda a: a.pts[-1][2])
                cut = max(2, len(t.pts) - rng.integers(3, 12))
                t.pts, t.apex = t.pts[:cut], False
                for a in self.axes:
                    if a.parent == self.axes.index(t) and a.at >= cut:
                        a.dead, a.alive, a.pts = True, False, a.pts[:1]
        # reiteration: a vigorous upper bough dedifferentiates into an orthotropic trunk
        if y > sp.reiter_age and self.nreiter < sp.reiter_max:
            cand = [i for i, a in enumerate(self.axes) if a.order == 1 and a.alive and not a.reiterated
                    and a.pts[0][2] > 0.5 * H and len(a.pts) > 3]
            for i in cand:
                if rng.random() < sp.reiter_rate:
                    a = self.axes[i]
                    a.reiterated, a.kind, a.apex, a.lat_age = True, 'ortho', True, 0
                    self.dir[i] = _norm(self.dir[i] * 0.3 + UP)
                    self.nreiter += 1

    # ---- outputs --------------------------------------------------------------------
    def segments(self):
        """(p0, p1, radius, order, dead) with pipe-rule radii."""
        sp = self.sp
        n = len(self.axes)
        kids = {i: [] for i in range(n)}
        for i, a in enumerate(self.axes):
            if a.parent >= 0:
                kids[a.parent].append(i)
        # leaf area proxy: count living tips; radius^2.4 ~ sum of tip weights outboard
        w_node = {}
        E = 2.4
        tipr = 0.004
        order = sorted(range(n), key=lambda i: -self.axes[i].born)

        def axis_r(i):
            a = self.axes[i]
            m = len(a.pts)
            acc = np.zeros(m)
            acc[-1] = tipr ** E if a.alive or a.apex else (tipr * 0.8) ** E
            # add children at their attachment
            for j in kids[i]:
                rj = w_node.get(j, tipr ** E)
                acc[min(a.at if False else self.axes[j].at, m - 1)] += rj
            # accumulate from tip to base; each year of age adds a ring
            r = np.cumsum(acc[::-1])[::-1]
            ages = np.array(a.age_nodes[:m]) if len(a.age_nodes) >= m else np.full(m, a.born)
            ring = (self.year - ages) * 0.0009
            rad = r ** (1 / E) + ring * (0.3 if a.order else 1.0)
            return rad
        rads = {}
        for i in order:
            rads[i] = axis_r(i)
            w_node[i] = rads[i][0] ** E
        segs = []
        for i, a in enumerate(self.axes):
            if len(a.pts) < 2:
                continue
            rr = rads[i]
            for k in range(len(a.pts) - 1):
                segs.append((a.pts[k], a.pts[k + 1], 0.5 * (rr[k] + rr[k + 1]), a.order, a.dead))
        return segs

    def foliage(self):
        """Clusters on the last needle_years of each living axis: (pos, r, normal, plagio)."""
        sp, out = self.sp, []
        for i, a in enumerate(self.axes):
            if a.dead or len(a.pts) < 2:
                continue
            if self.axes[a.parent].dead if a.parent >= 0 else False:
                continue
            ages = a.age_nodes
            for k in range(1, len(a.pts)):
                age = self.year - ages[k] if k < len(ages) else 0
                if age > sp.needle_years or (a.order == 0 and age > 1):
                    continue
                d = _norm(a.pts[k] - a.pts[k - 1])
                r = sp.cluster * (0.75 ** max(0, a.order - 1))
                if a.kind == 'plagio' and sp.pad:
                    nrm = _norm(UP - 0.4 * d[2] * d)
                    out.append((a.pts[k], r, nrm, True, d))
                else:
                    out.append((a.pts[k], r, UP, False, d))
        return out
Write: books/trees/src/diagram.py
"""Line diagrams of grown trees, in the manner of Hallé's key (side view, x-z plane)."""
import numpy as np
from PIL import Image, ImageDraw


def draw_tree(d, tree, ox, oy, s, leaves=True, ink=0, yaw=0.0):
    c, sn = np.cos(yaw), np.sin(yaw)

    def P(p):
        x = p[0] * c - p[1] * sn
        return (ox + x * s, oy - p[2] * s)
    for p0, p1, r, o, dead in tree.segments():
        w = max(1, int(round(2 * r * s)))
        d.line([P(p0), P(p1)], fill=(150 if dead else ink), width=w)
    if leaves:
        for pos, r, nrm, plag, dd in tree.foliage()[::3]:
            x, y = P(pos)
            rr = max(1.5, r * s * 0.5)
            if plag:
                d.ellipse([x - rr * 0.5, y - rr, x + rr * 0.5, y + rr * 0.2], outline=ink)
            else:
                d.ellipse([x - rr, y - rr, x + rr, y + rr], outline=ink)


def sequence(make, ages, s=6.0, W=None, H=None, label=""):
    """Grow one tree through ages and draw the architectures side by side on one soil line."""
    trees = []
    t = make()
    last = 0
    for a in ages:
        t.grow(a - last)
        last = a
        import copy
        trees.append(copy.deepcopy(t))
    hmax = max(tr.height() for tr in trees)
    widths = []
    for tr in trees:
        xs = [p[0] for ax in tr.axes for p in ax.pts]
        widths.append(max(4.0, max(xs) - min(xs)))
    Wd = int(sum(w + 3 for w in widths) * s) + 40 if W is None else W
    Hd = int(hmax * s) + 60 if H is None else H
    im = Image.new('L', (Wd, Hd), 255)
    d = ImageDraw.Draw(im)
    x = 20
    for tr, w in zip(trees, widths):
        xs = [p[0] for ax in tr.axes for p in ax.pts]
        ox = x + (-min(xs)) * s
        draw_tree(d, tr, ox, Hd - 30, s)
        d.text((x, Hd - 22), f"{tr.year} yr", fill=0)
        x += (w + 3) * s
    d.line([(0, Hd - 30), (Wd, Hd - 30)], fill=0)
    d.text((6, 4), label, fill=0)
    return im

cat >> src/arch.py <<'E'


def compact(tree):
    """Drop dead single-point axes (keeps indices valid by remapping parents)."""
    keep = [i for i, a in enumerate(tree.axes) if not (a.dead and len(a.pts) <= 1)]
    remap = {o: n for n, o in enumerate(keep)}
    axes, dirs = [], {}
    for o in keep:
        a = tree.axes[o]
        a.parent = remap.get(a.parent, -1) if a.parent >= 0 else -1
        axes.append(a)
        dirs[remap[o]] = tree.dir.get(o, UP)
    tree.axes, tree.dir = axes, dirs
    return tree

        """Gravity: plagiotropic boughs sag with their length and age; tips may turn up."""
        sp = self.sp
        moved = {}
        for i, a in enumerate(self.axes):
            if a.kind != 'plagio' or len(a.pts) < 2 or a.dead or a.order > 1:
                continue
            P = np.array(a.pts)
            seg = np.diff(P, axis=0)
            Ls = np.linalg.norm(seg, axis=1) + 1e-9
            tot = Ls.sum()
            s = np.cumsum(Ls)
            load = tot - s + Ls
            drop = sp.sag * load * (1 + 0.5 * a.order) * 0.1
            if sp.tip_up:
                drop = drop - sp.tip_up * 0.1 * np.clip(s - 0.7 * tot, 0, None)
            h = np.linalg.norm(seg[:, :2], axis=1) + 1e-9
            ang = np.clip(np.arctan2(seg[:, 2], h) - drop, -1.45, 1.2)
            hv = seg[:, :2] / h[:, None]
            v = np.stack([hv[:, 0] * np.cos(ang) * Ls, hv[:, 1] * np.cos(ang) * Ls, np.sin(ang) * Ls], 1)
            newP = np.vstack([P[:1], P[0] + np.cumsum(v, 0)])

            moved[i] = newP - P
            if a.apex:
                self.dir[i] = _norm(newP[-1] - newP[-2])

                if np.any(off):
